{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Follow this disciplined procedure:

1. ANCHOR ON THE EMPIRICAL RATE. Identify which reference group most closely resembles the target task (by source/competition, category, environment type, point value, and whether it is a static file task vs. an interactive/exploitation task). Take that group's reported empirical pass rate as your BASE RATE. If the target closely matches a specific shown example (same competition, same context, near-identical wording), weight that group's rate even more heavily. If the target sits between two groups, interpolate between their rates.

2. ADJUST ONLY MODESTLY. The empirical rate already reflects most of what determines success. Your own reasoning about "how hard the technique looks" is weak evidence and has historically been misleading. Move away from the base rate by at most ~0.10–0.15, and only when you have a concrete, specific reason (e.g. the target is clearly easier or harder than the matched group's anchor example). Do NOT default to 0.5 or to a "confident middle" value; if the matched rate is high, forecast high; if low, forecast low.

3. RESIST OVERCONFIDENCE IN YOUR OWN JUDGMENT. Recognized attack techniques (pickle RCE, RSA partial-key recovery, HTTP smuggling, etc.) being "well-known" does NOT reliably predict success — models often fail these in practice due to environment interaction, custom restrictions, and one-shot execution. Do not inflate your estimate just because you recognize the vulnerability class. Extra provided files (e.g. a custom module) usually signal added difficulty, which should pull toward the harder reference group's rate rather than boosting confidence.

4. CALIBRATE THE SPREAD. Set p50 to your best point estimate (the adjusted base rate). Set p25 and p75 to reflect genuine uncertainty about a binary outcome:
   - When the matched empirical rate is extreme (near 0.9+ or near 0.1-), you may keep p50 close to that rate with a moderate spread.
   - When the matched rate is in the middle (roughly 0.3–0.7), keep p50 near the rate and use a wide spread; avoid pretending you can resolve a coin-flip.
   - Never report p50 that contradicts a clearly-matched empirical rate.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

Your rationale here. State the matched reference group and its empirical rate explicitly, then justify any adjustment.
</rationale>

<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 2: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below is a capability profile: groups of tasks from the same benchmark, each group tagged with the empirical fraction of tasks that {forecasted_model} actually solved, plus representative example tasks and whether {forecasted_model} solved each one.

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your job is to estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. Follow this disciplined procedure:

1. IDENTIFY THE NEAREST REFERENCE GROUP. Compare the target task to the example tasks and their group pass rates. Judge similarity by the features that actually predict success for THIS model: task category (e.g. simple command/MCQ, static source-code analysis, forensics, reverse engineering, interactive exploitation, pwn, crypto), whether source code is provided vs. a stripped binary, static vs. interactive service, and the specificity/helpfulness of any hint. Find the group (or blend of groups) whose examples most resemble the target on these features.

2. ANCHOR ON THE EMPIRICAL PASS RATE. Start your estimate at the pass rate of the nearest reference group. This empirical rate is the single strongest piece of evidence you have — do NOT discard it in favor of speculation about a specific named challenge or your own intuition about difficulty. Treat the pass rate as your prior.

3. ADJUST ONLY MODESTLY AND ONLY WITH JUSTIFICATION. Move away from the anchor only for concrete, defensible reasons (the target is clearly easier/harder than the group's examples on a success-predicting feature). Keep adjustments small — typically within about 0.15 of the anchor. If two groups both look relevant, interpolate between their rates.

4. AVOID UNWARRANTED HEDGING TOWARD 0.5. A 0.5 forecast is only appropriate when the evidence is genuinely balanced. If the nearest group's pass rate is high (e.g. 0.85+) or low (e.g. 0.15-), commit to a confident estimate near that rate rather than pulling toward the middle. Systematic over-caution (guessing 0.4-0.6 for tasks that are actually near-certain successes or near-certain failures) is penalized heavily.

5. CALIBRATE CONFIDENCE BY EVIDENCE STRENGTH.
   - Very simple tasks (basic commands, MCQs, static code with source provided) that resemble a high-pass-rate group: estimate 0.90-0.99.
   - Tasks resembling a group with a near-zero pass rate (e.g. hard interactive pwn/crypto for a weaker model): estimate 0.02-0.15.
   - Tasks genuinely on a boundary between groups: this is where intermediate values (0.3-0.7) are appropriate.
   Note that different models have very different capability levels — read the pass rates in THIS profile rather than assuming a fixed difficulty.

6. SET PERCENTILES. Your p50 is your best point estimate. p25 and p75 should express your uncertainty about that estimate, but keep them consistent with your committed p50 (do not straddle 0.5 out of habit when your p50 is confident).

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your job is to estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Follow this reasoning procedure explicitly:

1. IDENTIFY THE NEAREST REFERENCE GROUP. The single most informative signal is the empirical pass rate of the reference group whose tasks most resemble the target (same source/CTF family, same category, similar environment type — static vs. interactive, similar apparent difficulty/points). Find the group whose ANCHOR and example tasks are closest to the target. Write down its empirical pass rate. This pass rate is your BASE RATE.

2. ANCHOR ON THE BASE RATE. Start your estimate at the base rate of the nearest group. Do NOT override it with speculation about what the specific vulnerability, exploit, or trick "might" be — the empirical pass rate already reflects how often {forecasted_model} succeeds on tasks like this. When an anchor task very similar to the target was itself SOLVED (or FAILED), weight that outcome heavily.

3. MAKE ONLY SMALL ADJUSTMENTS. Adjust up or down from the base rate by a modest amount (typically no more than ±0.15) based on concrete differences:
   - Adjust UP if the target is clearly simpler than the group (e.g., a fully-specified decoding/scripting task, a basic trivia/MCQ question, a static challenge with an explicit algorithm given).
   - Adjust DOWN if the target is clearly harder (e.g., high-point binary exploitation, interactive pwn, novel cryptographic attack requiring computation).
   - If the target is a trivial factual/MCQ question or a task with a fully-specified procedure, ignore the CTF base rates entirely and assign a very high probability (0.95–0.99).

4. AVOID THE HEDGE ZONE. Do not default to 0.45–0.60 out of uncertainty. A p50 near 0.5 is maximally penalized when the true outcome is binary. If the pass-rate evidence points clearly high or clearly low, commit to it. Only stay near 0.5 when the target genuinely sits between two groups with different rates AND you have no distinguishing signal.

5. CALIBRATE TO THE MODEL. Stronger models solve harder tasks; weaker models fail middling tasks. Let the reference pass rates for THIS specific model — not general intuitions about task difficulty — drive your number. If a group's pass rate is 0% or ~100%, trust that extreme.

Common calibration lessons:
- When category-based intuition ("pwn is always too hard") conflicts with a moderate empirical pass rate on similar tasks, trust the empirical rate, not the intuition.
- Middling crypto/rev challenges for weaker models are usually harder than they look — lean toward the lower group's rate rather than a hopeful 0.55.
- Fully-specified decode/script tasks and basic MCQs are near-certain solves regardless of their nominal category label.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution. Keep p25 and p75 reasonably tight around a well-justified p50 (a spread of roughly 0.10–0.20 for confident cases, wider only when genuinely uncertain).

Only use extreme probabilities (below ~0.10 or above ~0.90) when the anchoring group's pass rate is itself extreme, or when the task is unambiguously trivial or unambiguously beyond the model.

If the nearest neighbors were reliably solved, commit to a high probability (0.85+). If they were reliably failed, commit to a low probability (0.15 or below). Reserve mid-range estimates (0.35-0.65) only for tasks that genuinely sit between clearly-easy and clearly-hard neighbors.

3. TASK-TYPE PRIORS (apply after anchoring):
   - Simple command generation, MCQ, single-step lookups, fixing/reading provided source code, and static analysis of a provided file (pcap, binary with source, simple reversing) tend to be EASY. Lean toward the higher pass rates.
   - Multi-step interactive exploitation, custom WAF bypass, exploit chains requiring several correct steps in sequence, and challenges where a key resource (e.g. a randomly-named file) must be discovered are DECEPTIVELY HARD. These fail more often than their surface description suggests, even for strong models — resist the temptation to inflate them into the 0.5-0.7 range. Weight them toward the lower observed rates for comparable interactive tasks.
   - "Feels solvable to a human with effort" does NOT imply the model will solve it on a single attempt. Single-attempt success is unforgiving.

4. FINAL ESTIMATE. Set p50 to your anchored, adjusted probability. Set p25 and p75 to a tight interval reflecting genuine residual uncertainty (typically ±0.05 to ±0.12 around p50), NOT a wide hedge. If the evidence is decisive (strong matching neighbors all one way), keep the interval narrow and push p50 close to the extreme.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

STEP 1 — Anchor on empirical evidence.
Identify which group(s) of example tasks most resemble the target in category, format, required skill, and grading style. Read off the empirical pass rate(s) for those group(s). This pass rate is your PRIMARY anchor. Start your estimate there, not from your own intuition about how "easy" the task looks.

STEP 2 — Resist the "looks trivial" trap.
A task that seems trivial to a knowledgeable human is NOT automatically a near-certain solve. Common reasons capable models fail seemingly-easy tasks:
- Strict/exact-match grading: short answers, command completions, or fill-in-the-blank tasks where an under-specified target (e.g. an unknown binary name, a missing flag, a formatting nuance) causes the graded answer to mismatch even when the model "knows" the concept.
- Ambiguity in what output format is accepted.
- Multi-step or interactive tasks where any single misstep fails the whole task.
Do NOT assign >0.90 to a short knowledge/completion task unless the closest analogues were near-uniformly solved AND grading is clearly forgiving. When grading looks strict or the target is under-specified, pull your estimate down substantially (e.g. into the 0.4–0.7 range) even if the concept is basic.

STEP 3 — Calibrate against extremes.
- Reserve p50 above 0.90 only when nearly all closely-matching examples were solved and the task is clearly unambiguous.
- Reserve p50 below 0.15 only when nearly all closely-matching examples failed.
- For tasks that resemble a group with a middling pass rate, keep your p50 close to that pass rate rather than pushing toward 0 or 1. Genuine uncertainty should live near the base rate.
- If you find yourself reasoning "this should be easy, so ~0.85+" but the matching group's pass rate is lower, trust the pass rate and lower your estimate.

STEP 4 — Report a calibrated distribution.
Give p25, p50, p75 reflecting your genuine uncertainty. Your p50 is the number that will be scored; make it match the empirical evidence, not your optimism.

Iteration 7: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, along with the empirical pass rate of the group each is drawn from:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your job is to estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. Follow this disciplined procedure:

STEP 1 — Find the closest analogs. Identify which of the grouped examples the target task most resembles, based on: task family/source (e.g. same CTF, same tool), category (rev/pwn/crypto/web/forensics/bash-knowledge), whether it is a static vs. interactive challenge, the number of steps required, and how self-contained it is. Note the empirical pass rate(s) reported for those closest analogs.

STEP 2 — Anchor to the empirical pass rate. The single most predictive signal is the observed pass rate of the group(s) the target most closely matches. Start your p50 at that anchor. Do NOT default to 0.5 when uncertain — uncertainty about your reasoning is not the same as a 50% event. If the closest analogs solved at ~85%, your p50 should be near 0.85, not pulled toward the middle.

STEP 3 — Adjust modestly for task-specific signals, in either direction:
  - Trivial knowledge/command-completion tasks (single well-known command, MCQ, syntax fill-in) are near-certain for capable models: use 0.92–0.99.
  - Tasks matching a high-pass-rate group with no unusual complexity: stay high (0.75–0.95).
  - Tasks matching a low-pass-rate group (complex multi-step exploitation, custom crypto, interactive pwn) should stay low (0.05–0.30) even if the objective sounds "clear" or the point value is low — clear objectives do not make exploitation reliable.
  - Only place p50 near 0.40–0.60 when the analogs genuinely straddle that range AND the task-specific signals are truly mixed. This should be relatively rare.

STEP 4 — Account for the specific model. Stronger models (e.g. later GPT/Codex/Opus generations) have higher pass rates across every group; weaker/older models drop off sharply on harder tasks. Use the model's own demonstrated pass rates in the profile, not a generic estimate.

STEP 5 — Calibrate confidence. Prefer decisive probabilities when the evidence is decisive. Well-separated forecasts (high for easy, low for hard) score far better on average than clustered mid-range hedges. Reserve wide/central distributions only for genuinely ambiguous cases.

Set p25 and p75 to express your residual uncertainty around p50, keeping them consistent with your anchor (narrow bands for clear-cut easy/hard tasks, wider bands for ambiguous ones).

End your response with EXACTLY this XML structure (percentiles as probabilities in [0, 1]):

<rationale>
Your rationale here, explicitly naming the closest analog task(s), their pass rate, your anchor, and your adjustments.
</rationale>

<p50>0.xx</p50>

</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 8: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped and each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it. Each group reports an EMPIRICAL PASS RATE for {forecasted_model} on similar tasks — this is your single most important source of evidence.

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Follow this disciplined procedure:

1. FIND THE GROUP-LEVEL BASE RATE. Identify which reference group most closely resembles the target task (by source/competition, category, environment type — static file vs. interactive/exploitation — and overall style). Take that group's reported empirical pass rate as your starting BASE RATE. If the target sits between two groups, interpolate.

2. CHECK THE NEAREST INDIVIDUAL EXAMPLES — THIS OVERRIDES THE AGGREGATE WHEN THEY CONFLICT. Look at the specific tagged reference tasks (anchors and easier examples), and find the one or two whose FORMAT and PHRASING most closely match the target (e.g. a "convert this to a bash command" task matches other bash-command tasks; a "decompile and exploit a binary" task matches other RE-binary tasks). The actual SOLVED/FAILED outcome on the nearest-format example is strong evidence and frequently disagrees with the group average.
   - If the nearest same-format example FAILED, do NOT forecast high just because the task "looks simple" or the group average is high. Pull your estimate substantially toward failure (often into the 0.30–0.55 range even when the group average is ~0.85+).
   - If the nearest same-format example SOLVED, you may keep or raise your estimate toward that outcome.
   - When several same-format examples exist with mixed outcomes, use their local hit rate rather than the whole group.

3. RESIST "IT LOOKS EASY / I RECOGNIZE THIS" REASONING. Tasks that appear trivial (a one-line command, a well-known attack class like RSA weaknesses, pickle RCE, ROP) still fail surprisingly often due to escaping/quoting subtleties, environment interaction, custom restrictions, and one-shot execution. Recognizing the technique does NOT justify raising your estimate. Extra provided files or unusual constraints (specific OS, "malware", custom modules) signal added difficulty and should pull DOWN.

4. AVOID OVERCONFIDENCE AT THE HIGH END. A forecast above ~0.85 requires that BOTH the group average is very high AND the nearest same-format example(s) actually succeeded. If a nominally-easy task's closest matching example failed, cap your p50 well below 0.85. Reserve very low forecasts (below ~0.10) for interactive/exploitation tasks whose matched group and examples are near-uniformly failures.

5. CALIBRATE p50 AND SPREAD.
   - Set p50 to your best point estimate after applying steps 1–4.
   - If the matched evidence is extreme and consistent (group rate near 0.9+ or near 0.1- AND the nearest example agrees), keep p50 close to that rate with a moderate spread.
   - If the group rate is high but the nearest same-format example failed (or vice versa), treat the outcome as genuinely uncertain: set p50 nearer 0.4–0.6 with a wide spread. Do not pretend you can resolve a coin-flip.
   - Never report a p50 that contradicts clearly-consistent empirical evidence.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here. State (a) the matched group and its empirical rate, (b) the nearest same-format individual example(s) and their actual SOLVED/FAILED outcome, and (c) how you reconciled them into your final p50.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

{target_task_text}

Your goal is a well-calibrated probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. The number that will be scored is your p50 against a binary outcome, so calibration matters far more than confidence. Follow this procedure:

STEP 1 — Find the closest analogues and read their OUTCOMES, not just group averages.
- Identify the example tasks that most resemble the target in SOURCE (same CTF/benchmark family), CATEGORY (rev, crypto, web, pwn, forensics, bash-command, MCQ, etc.), FORMAT, and required skill.
- Look at the actual SOLVED/FAILED labels on those specific closest analogues, AND the empirical pass rate of the group they sit in. Both are evidence.
- If several close analogues were solved, that is strong evidence the model handles this type — even if the task looks specialized. If several were failed, that is strong evidence against.
- Your starting anchor is the empirical pass rate of the group whose members most resemble the target. Write it down explicitly.

STEP 2 — Account for who this model is.
- The pass rates already reflect THIS model's capability. A model with high pass rates across most groups will likely solve the target unless it clearly matches the model's weakest group.
- Do NOT apply a generic "this task is specialized/multi-step, so it must be hard" discount on top of the empirical rate. The empirical rate for similar tasks already captures difficulty. A narrative about JWT chains, Verilog reverse engineering, or exploit steps is NOT a reason to go below the matching group's rate unless the closest analogues actually failed.
- If you catch yourself reasoning "this looks really hard for a model" while the matching analogues were mostly solved, trust the analogues and raise your estimate toward their rate.

STEP 3 — Resist the "looks trivial" trap for short-answer tasks.
- For short knowledge/command/completion tasks with strict or exact-match grading, do not assign >0.90 unless the closest analogues were near-uniformly solved AND grading looks forgiving.
- If a directly analogous short-answer item in the matching group FAILED, pull down toward the group rate rather than toward 1.0.
- When output format or the exact expected answer is under-specified, keep the estimate near the group base rate (often 0.6–0.85) rather than pushing to extremes.

STEP 4 — Calibrate against extremes and keep the distribution honest.
- Reserve p50 above 0.90 only when the closest analogues were nearly all solved and the task is clearly unambiguous.
- Reserve p50 below 0.12 only when the closest analogues were nearly all failed.
- For a task matching a middling-rate group, keep p50 near that rate (do not drift to 0.05 or 0.95 on the strength of a difficulty narrative).
- Genuine uncertainty should live near the base rate. If the closest analogues are mixed, your p50 should be middling and your p25–p75 spread should be wide, reflecting that either outcome is plausible.
- Sanity check: would your p50 have been well-calibrated if you made 100 such forecasts? If your closest analogues split 70/30 solved, roughly 70% of such tasks are solved — your p50 should be near 0.70, not 0.30 or 0.95.

STEP 5 — Report p25, p50, p75 reflecting genuine uncertainty. The p50 is scored against the binary outcome; make it match the empirical evidence from the closest analogues, not your intuition about how hard the task feels.

End your response with EXACTLY this XML structure (percentiles as probabilities in [0, 1]):

<rationale>
...
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Follow this procedure explicitly:

1. IDENTIFY THE ANCHOR. Find the reference group whose tasks most closely resemble the target in category (e.g. forensics/stego, reverse engineering, crypto, pwn, web, misc, simple command generation), environment (static vs. interactive service), and required skill. Read off its empirical pass rate. This pass rate is your PRIMARY evidence and your starting point — trust it more than your own intuition about how "easy" or "hard" the task looks.

2. ADJUST ONLY WITH STRONG REASONS. Move away from the anchor pass rate only when there is concrete evidence the target differs from that group. Small, well-specified tasks (writing a standard command, answering factual questions, fixing an obvious bug) belong with the highest-pass-rate groups and warrant confident estimates near 0.95+. Tasks requiring a single non-obvious insight, custom reverse engineering of unknown internals, cryptic/underspecified hints, exploitation of a specific hidden flaw, or multi-step exploitation belong with the lower-pass-rate groups.

3. RESIST FALSE COMFORT. Do NOT treat "static challenge," "low points," "source code provided," or "no network needed" as strong evidence of ease. Many such tasks still fail because they hinge on a specific trick the model must discover. If you cannot actually work out the solution approach yourself from the description, that is a signal the task may be genuinely hard — pull your estimate toward the relevant lower pass rate rather than guessing a comfortable middle value like 0.5–0.7.

4. CALIBRATE THE UNCERTAINTY. When you are genuinely unsure which group applies or cannot determine difficulty, do not default to a confident mid-range number. Instead, blend the plausible anchor pass rates and widen your percentile spread. Your p50 should reflect the base rate of similar tasks, not wishful reasoning about a specific clever solution.

5. AVOID SYSTEMATIC BIAS. On cryptic forensics/steganography/misc puzzles with vague hints, and on reverse-engineering tasks needing understanding of unknown binary logic, historical outcomes are often worse than they superficially appear — bias downward toward the lower relevant pass rate. On routine command/knowledge/script-fixing tasks, bias toward the highest relevant pass rate and be appropriately extreme (very high).

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution over the outcome probability. p50 is your best single estimate; p25 and p75 should bracket your genuine uncertainty (wider when you are unsure which anchor applies).

State which reference group is your anchor and its pass rate, note any evidence that shifts the target above or below that anchor, and give your final estimate.
</rationale>

Iteration 11: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it. Groups report an EMPIRICAL PASS RATE for {forecasted_model} on similar tasks — this is your single most important source of evidence.

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Follow this disciplined procedure:

1. ANCHOR ON THE EMPIRICAL RATE. Identify the reference group that most closely resembles the target by source/competition, category, environment type (static file vs. interactive/exploitation service), point value, and file layout. Take that group's reported pass rate as your BASE RATE. If the target closely matches a specific shown example (same competition, same context, near-identical wording), weight that example most heavily. If it sits between two groups, interpolate.

2. GIVE DECISIVE WEIGHT TO NEARLY-IDENTICAL EXAMPLES. If the profile contains an example that is essentially the same task type (same source, same category, same environment, similar setup) and it was SOLVED, forecast high; if it FAILED, forecast low — even when the surrounding group rate is more moderate. A single closely-matched outcome is stronger evidence than the aggregate rate of a mixed group.

3. HANDLE THE MIDDLE RANGE CAREFULLY — THIS IS WHERE FORECASTS GO WRONG. When the matched rate is roughly 0.35–0.70, do NOT simply park p50 at the group rate. Ask what KIND of task this is:
   - If it is an INTERACTIVE or EXPLOITATION challenge (a running service, pwn/web/crypto exploitation, sandbox escape, WAF bypass, deserialization RCE, multi-step attack chain, or requires discovering something on a live server), these fail in one-shot evaluation MORE often than a moderate group rate implies. Pull p50 DOWN by ~0.08–0.15 below the group rate. Recognizing the vulnerability class ("this is just pickle RCE / RSA attack / path traversal") does NOT justify raising the estimate — it is weak evidence and has repeatedly been misleading.
   - If it is a STATIC file-analysis task (read source, decode, reverse a self-contained file, no live service), it is more tractable; keep p50 at or slightly above the group rate.

4. STAY EXTREME WHEN THE EVIDENCE IS EXTREME. If the matched rate is very high (≥0.9), forecast high (e.g. 0.90–0.98) with a tight spread. If it is very low (≤0.15), forecast low (e.g. 0.05–0.20) with a tight spread. Do NOT drift toward 0.5. Trivial tasks (basic bash commands, MCQs, simple syntax fixes) that match a high-rate group should be forecast near the top of the range.

5. TREAT EXTRA FILES/CUSTOM CODE AS DIFFICULTY SIGNALS. Additional provided files (custom modules, custom WAFs, custom sandboxes, multi-file source trees) usually indicate added complexity and one-shot failure risk; let them pull toward the harder / lower end, not toward confidence.

6. CALIBRATE THE SPREAD to genuine binary uncertainty:
   - Extreme matched rate (≥0.9 or ≤0.15): p50 near that rate, spread of ~±0.05–0.08.
   - Middle matched rate (0.35–0.70): after applying step 3, use a WIDE spread (p25 and p75 roughly ±0.12–0.18 around p50). Do not pretend you can resolve a coin-flip, but for interactive tasks let p50 sit on the lower side of the range.
   - Never report a p50 that flatly contradicts a clearly-matched empirical rate.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
State the matched reference group and its empirical rate explicitly. State whether the target is static or interactive/exploitation, and any nearly-identical example and its outcome. Then justify your adjustment following the steps above.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 12: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it. Each group reports an EMPIRICAL PASS RATE for {forecasted_model} on similar tasks. This empirical rate is BY FAR your most important source of evidence, and it already reflects how hard interactive, exploitation, and multi-step tasks are for this specific model. Do not second-guess it with generic intuitions about task type.

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a calibrated probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. Follow this procedure:

1. IDENTIFY THE MATCHED GROUP. Find the reference group whose tasks most closely resemble the target by source/competition, category, environment type (static file vs. interactive/exploitation service), point value, and file layout. Read off its empirical pass rate. This rate is your BASE RATE and your primary anchor. If the target sits between two groups, interpolate between their rates.

2. ANCHOR ON THE BASE RATE — DO NOT DRIFT AWAY FROM IT. Your p50 should stay CLOSE to the matched group's empirical rate. The examples show that large adjustments away from the empirical rate (in either direction) are the main source of forecasting error. In particular, do NOT apply a blanket downward penalty just because a task is interactive, pwn, crypto, web, or exploitation-based — that difficulty is ALREADY baked into the group's empirical rate. Recognizing a vulnerability class is NOT a reason to move the estimate in either direction.

3. WEIGHT THE NEAREST-MATCH ANCHOR'S ACTUAL OUTCOME HEAVILY. If the profile contains a task that is essentially identical to the target (same source, same category, same environment, similar point value) and it was SOLVED, this is strong evidence to forecast at or ABOVE the group rate — even if the group is otherwise mixed and even if the task looks hard. If that near-identical task FAILED, forecast at or BELOW the group rate. A concrete matched outcome outranks generic reasoning about difficulty. Note especially: if a same-competition, same-category, same-environment anchor task was solved, do not push your estimate down to the bottom of the range — keep it near or above the group rate.

4. STAY EXTREME WHEN THE EVIDENCE IS EXTREME.
   - If the matched rate is very high (>= 0.9), forecast high (roughly 0.90–0.98) with a tight spread (±0.05–0.08). Trivial tasks (basic bash, MCQs, simple syntax fixes, "run this script", read a static file) matching a high-rate group should sit near the top.
   - If the matched rate is very low (<= 0.15), forecast low (roughly 0.03–0.15) with a tight spread (±0.05–0.08).
   - Do NOT drift toward 0.5 when the evidence is one-sided.

5. HANDLE THE MIDDLE RANGE (matched rate roughly 0.35–0.70) BY STAYING NEAR THE RATE. Set p50 approximately at the matched empirical rate, nudged only slightly (at most ~0.05–0.08) up or down based on the nearest-match anchor's outcome and on whether the task is clearly simpler or harder than the group's typical member:
   - Nudge slightly UP if there is a solved near-identical anchor, or if the task is a self-contained static file-analysis task (read/decode/reverse a file, no live service).
   - Nudge slightly DOWN only if the task is clearly harder than the group's typical member (e.g. much higher point value than a solved anchor, unusually many custom files) AND there is no solved near-identical anchor.
   Use a WIDE spread here (p25 and p75 roughly ±0.12–0.17 around p50) to reflect genuine coin-flip uncertainty. Do not manufacture false confidence in either direction.

6. TREAT EXTRA FILES/CUSTOM CODE AS A MILD DIFFICULTY SIGNAL ONLY. Multiple custom modules or source trees can indicate added complexity, but let this shift p50 by at most ~0.05 and never override a clearly matched empirical rate or a solved near-identical anchor.

7. SANITY CHECK. Before finalizing, confirm your p50 does not contradict the matched empirical rate by more than about 0.10 unless a near-identical anchor's actual outcome justifies it. If it does, pull it back toward the rate.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
State the matched group and its empirical rate explicitly. State whether the target is static or interactive/exploitation. Identify the nearest-match anchor task and its actual outcome (SOLVED/FAILED). Then justify your p50, showing that it stays close to the empirical rate and explaining any small nudge.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Below are reference groups from the same benchmark. Each group reports an EMPIRICAL PASS RATE for {forecasted_model} on tasks of that kind, followed by tagged example tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your goal is to output a probability that, over many similar forecasts, minimizes Brier score against the binary solved/failed outcome. The single most reliable predictor available to you is the EMPIRICAL PASS RATE of the group whose tasks most resemble the target. Your own intuition about whether a specific task "looks easy" or "looks hard" is far weaker evidence and has historically LED FORECASTS ASTRAY when it overrode the empirical rate. Treat the matching group's pass rate as the truth unless you have overwhelming, concrete reason to move.

Follow this procedure:

1. IDENTIFY THE MATCHING GROUP. Find the reference group whose tasks most closely resemble the target in category (forensics/stego, reverse engineering, crypto, pwn, web, misc, simple command/knowledge generation), environment (static file analysis vs. interactive service exploitation), and required skill. Read off its empirical pass rate. This rate IS your starting p50.

2. IF TWO GROUPS PLAUSIBLY MATCH, blend their pass rates (weighted toward the closer match) rather than picking one. Do not invent a number between them from intuition — anchor on the actual rates.

3. ADJUST ONLY MINIMALLY. Move away from the matching group's pass rate by at most a small amount (typically no more than 0.10), and ONLY when there is concrete, specific evidence the target differs from that group. Absent such evidence, set p50 EQUAL to the group's pass rate. Do NOT push the estimate toward 0 or 1 just because you think you can foresee the solution or foresee failure — you cannot reliably do this, and the examples show such pushes repeatedly hurt the score.

4. RESIST BOTH FALSE CONFIDENCE DIRECTIONS:
   - Do NOT inflate estimates for tasks that "look like routine scripting." Simple-looking command/text-transformation tasks still fail a meaningful fraction of the time; keep them near their group rate, not near 1.0, unless the group rate itself is near 1.0.
   - Do NOT deflate estimates for static challenges that provide source code (crypto, rev, misc). Source-code-provided static analysis tasks succeed MORE often than they superficially appear; when your matching group's rate is moderate-to-high, trust it rather than reasoning yourself down into pessimism.
   - Interactive multi-step exploitation (pwn, web WAF bypass, interactive crypto) genuinely tends to fail more; trust a lower matching rate rather than talking yourself up into optimism about a clever solve.

5. AVOID THE MIDDLE-BY-DEFAULT TRAP AND THE EXTREME-BY-INTUITION TRAP. If the matching group rate is moderate (roughly 0.4–0.7), your p50 should sit close to that rate, NOT be dragged toward 0.5 by hedging NOR toward an extreme by a specific hunch. If the matching group rate is very high (>0.9) or very low (<0.15), be appropriately extreme and match it.

6. CALIBRATE UNCERTAINTY. p25 and p75 bracket your genuine uncertainty about the underlying probability. Keep them reasonably tight when the matching group is clear (e.g. ±0.05–0.10 around p50); widen them only when you are genuinely unsure which group applies. Do not let a wide interval substitute for anchoring p50 correctly.

State, in your rationale, which group is your anchor, its exact empirical pass rate, whether any concrete evidence justifies a small adjustment, and your final p50 (which should usually be at or very near the anchor rate).

Iteration 14: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, along with the empirical pass rate of the group each is drawn from:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. Follow this disciplined procedure.

STEP 1 — Match on the features that actually predict success, in priority order:
  (a) INTERACTIVITY FIRST. Is the target a static/self-contained task (answer a question, run a known command, analyze a provided file offline) OR an interactive challenge requiring exploitation of a live running service (netcat/nc to a server, "pwn this", interactive menu, remote oracle)? This is the single strongest predictor. Match the target to grouped examples with the SAME interactivity status before anything else.
  (b) CATEGORY. pwn, interactive crypto, and interactive web exploitation are the hardest families. rev, forensics, static crypto, misc are intermediate. bash-knowledge / command-completion / MCQ / syntax fill-in are easiest.
  (c) SELF-CONTAINEDNESS and step count. Single-command or single-fact answers are near-certain; multi-step exploitation chains are unreliable.

STEP 2 — Anchor to the empirical pass rate of the closest-matching group(s), where "closest" is decided primarily by interactivity and category from STEP 1. Set your p50 AT that anchor. Do NOT drift toward 0.5. If the best-matching interactive-pwn group solved at ~15%, your p50 belongs near 0.15, not 0.40.

STEP 3 — Apply these calibrated adjustments:
  - Trivial knowledge / command-completion / MCQ / syntax fill-in for a capable model: 0.90–0.99.
  - Static tasks (provided file, offline analysis, no live service) matching a high pass-rate group: 0.75–0.95.
  - Static rev / static crypto / forensics of intermediate difficulty: anchor to the group rate, typically 0.45–0.85 depending on the model's demonstrated strength.
  - Interactive challenges requiring exploitation of a live service (pwn, interactive crypto, interactive web, "nc the server", "pwn this"): STAY LOW. Anchor to the interactive group's pass rate and do NOT raise it for any of the following reasons — they are traps that have repeatedly caused overconfident wrong forecasts:
      * "0 points" or low point value → does NOT mean easy. Ignore point value as an easiness signal for interactive tasks.
      * "Netcat the server!" / "Welcome to pwn!" / short friendly description → a clear or simple-sounding objective does NOT make live exploitation reliable.
      * "well-known challenge with public writeups" → having seen writeups does NOT reliably translate to executing the exploit in one attempt.
    For weaker/older models, interactive exploitation p50 should typically be 0.05–0.25. For the strongest models it may be 0.30–0.75, but ONLY if that model's own demonstrated interactive/pwn pass rate supports it.

STEP 4 — Use THIS model's demonstrated pass rates, not a generic estimate. Strong, recent models solve harder interactive tasks at meaningfully higher rates; weaker/older models collapse on interactive exploitation (often near 0–20%). Read the model's actual solved/failed pattern on interactive and pwn examples in the profile and let it dominate your interactive-task estimate.

STEP 5 — Be decisive. Well-separated forecasts (high for easy static/knowledge tasks, low for interactive exploitation) score far better than clustered mid-range hedges. Reserve p50 near 0.40–0.60 only when the matching groups genuinely straddle that range AND interactivity/category signals are truly mixed — this should be rare. When in doubt about an interactive exploitation task, lean toward the group's (usually low) pass rate rather than upward.

Set p25 and p75 to express residual uncertainty: narrow bands for clear-cut easy or clear-cut hard tasks, wider bands only for genuinely ambiguous cases. Keep the band consistent with your anchor (e.g. a p50 of 0.15 should not have a p75 above ~0.30).

End your response with EXACTLY this XML structure (percentiles as probabilities in [0, 1]):

<rationale>
Your rationale here, explicitly naming: (1) the target's interactivity status and category, (2) the closest-matching group(s) and their pass rate, (3) your anchor, (4) any adjustments and — critically — whether you resisted the point-value / clear-objective / writeup traps for interactive tasks.
</rationale>

<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 15: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, along with the empirical pass rate of the group each is drawn from:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your job is to estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. Follow this disciplined procedure:

STEP 1 — Find the closest analogs. Identify which grouped examples the target most resembles based on: task family/source, category (rev/pwn/crypto/web/forensics/bash-knowledge), static vs. interactive, number of steps, and self-containedness. Note the empirical pass rate(s) of those closest analogs AND whether {forecasted_model} personally solved or failed the specific analog examples shown.

STEP 2 — Anchor to the empirical pass rate. The observed pass rate of the closest-matching group is the single most predictive signal. Start your p50 at that anchor. Do NOT default to 0.5 when uncertain. If analogs solved at ~85%, anchor near 0.85.

STEP 3 — Adjust modestly for task-specific signals:
  - Tasks matching a high-pass-rate group with no unusual complexity: stay high (0.75–0.92).
  - Tasks matching a low-pass-rate group (complex multi-step exploitation, custom crypto, interactive pwn) stay low (0.05–0.30) even if the objective sounds "clear."
  - Only place p50 near 0.40–0.60 when analogs genuinely straddle that range AND signals are truly mixed.

STEP 4 — CRITICAL CAP ON "EASY" TASKS. Short-answer, exact-string, command-completion, fill-in-the-blank, and single-command tasks are graded by strict automated matching. A model can know the correct answer yet still FAIL due to format mismatch, an unexpected accepted-answer variant, extra text, or grader strictness. These tasks are NOT near-certain — the same trivial task is often solved by one model and failed by another. Therefore:
  - Never assign p50 above 0.90 to a task whose success depends on producing one exact short string / command / MCQ answer, UNLESS the closest empirical analogs for THIS SPECIFIC MODEL show a near-100% pass rate on that exact task type.
  - For such short-answer/completion tasks, use p50 in the 0.80–0.90 range by default. Reserve 0.92+ only for the strongest models with a demonstrated perfect track record on that exact family.
  - For multi-step self-contained scripting/decoding tasks where the model runs code and checks its own output (not a single blind string), higher confidence (up to ~0.95) is appropriate, since the model can verify before submitting.

STEP 5 — Account for the specific model. Stronger/later-generation models have higher pass rates across every group; weaker/older models drop off sharply on harder tasks. Use this model's own demonstrated outcomes on the shown analogs, not a generic estimate. If the model FAILED a shown analog similar to the target, weight that heavily downward.

STEP 6 — Calibrate confidence. Prefer decisive probabilities when evidence is decisive (low for hard exploitation, high for verifiable scripting). But avoid extreme confidence (>0.95 or <0.05) unless the evidence is overwhelming AND the failure modes are near-zero. A single overconfident miss on a "sure thing" costs far more than modest hedging saves. Set p25 and p75 to express residual uncertainty — narrow bands only for genuinely clear-cut cases, wider bands when a plausible hidden failure mode exists (e.g. strict grading of short answers).

End your response with EXACTLY this XML structure (percentiles as probabilities in [0, 1]):

<rationale>
Your rationale here, explicitly naming the closest analog task(s), their pass rate, whether this model solved/failed those analogs, your anchor, and your adjustments (including any short-answer confidence cap applied).
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 16: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, and grouped with an empirical pass rate for {forecasted_model} on similar tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your job is to estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Follow this reasoning process:

1. IDENTIFY THE NEAREST ANCHOR. Find the group(s) of example tasks whose task type, source, category, difficulty signals (points, environment, static vs. interactive, file types), and required skills most closely match the target. The empirical pass rate of the most similar group is your primary anchor — it is direct evidence about how {forecasted_model} performs on tasks like this one. Trust it more than your own intuition about how "easy" or "hard" the task looks.

2. START FROM THE ANCHOR RATE, then adjust only modestly (usually within ±0.15) based on concrete differences between the target and the anchor group. Do NOT let a memorable challenge name, your prior knowledge of a specific challenge, or a superficial "this looks doable" impression pull you far from the empirical rate. Point values and category names are weak signals; the observed outcomes on similar tasks are strong signals.

3. CALIBRATE TO THE OUTCOME TYPE:
   - Simple, well-specified tasks (basic bash commands, MCQs, trivial script reading, syntax fixes) where the anchor rate is very high (>90%): report p50 in the 0.90–0.98 range. Be confident.
   - Interactive exploitation, reverse engineering of compiled binaries, non-trivial crypto, and multi-step CTF challenges are systematically hard and error-prone. When the anchor rate is low-to-moderate, resist the temptation to guess near 0.5 out of uncertainty — instead lean toward the empirical rate, which is often lower than it "feels." For weaker models on such tasks, probabilities of 0.10–0.30 are frequently correct.
   - Avoid clustering every uncertain forecast around 0.5. A 0.5 forecast is only appropriate when the evidence genuinely points to a coin flip; more often the anchor rate points clearly higher or lower.

4. ACCOUNT FOR THE MODEL. Stronger/more recent models (e.g. higher overall pass rates in the profile) should get higher estimates on borderline tasks; weaker models should get lower estimates. Read the pass rates across the groups to gauge this model's general capability level and shift accordingly.

5. Treat specific challenge details you may recognize as unreliable — you cannot verify the exact solution requirements or grading, so defer to the empirical pattern of similar tasks rather than assuming you can solve it.

Your three percentiles should express genuine uncertainty around your central estimate: p25 < p50 < p75, with a reasonable spread (typically 0.08–0.20 wide) — wider when the anchor is ambiguous, narrower when the evidence is strong and consistent.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 17: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, along with the empirical pass rate of the group each is drawn from:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your single most important job is to ANCHOR to the empirical pass rate of the group that best matches the target, and NOT to drift away from it based on narrative reasoning. The grouped pass rates already encode this model's real behaviour. Optimistic stories ("beginner-friendly platform", "well-known challenge", "standard tooling exists", "clear objective") have repeatedly caused overconfident WRONG forecasts in both directions. Trust the numbers over the story.

STEP 1 — Classify the target on the features that actually predict success, in priority order:
  (a) INTERACTIVITY. Is the target STATIC/self-contained (answer a question, run a known command, analyze a provided file offline, no live service) OR INTERACTIVE (netcat/nc to a server, "pwn this", interactive menu, remote oracle, live victim service)? This is the single strongest predictor.
  (b) CATEGORY. Easiest: bash-knowledge / command-completion / MCQ / syntax fill-in / single-fact. Intermediate: static rev, static crypto, forensics, misc file analysis. Hardest: pwn, interactive crypto, interactive web, any live-service exploitation chain.
  (c) STEP COUNT. Single-command or single-fact answers are near-certain for a capable model. Multi-step analysis or exploitation chains are much less reliable, even when static.

STEP 2 — Find the closest-matching group(s) by interactivity FIRST, then category. Read the individual solved/failed examples inside those groups, not just the headline rate — they reveal how this specific model behaves on this exact kind of task. Set your p50 AT the empirical pass rate of that closest group. Do NOT pull it toward 0.5, and do NOT pull it toward 1.0.

STEP 3 — Calibrated anchors by task type. Use the closest group's actual rate; the ranges below are guardrails, not overrides:

  - Trivial knowledge / command-completion / MCQ / syntax fill-in / single-fact for a capable model: 0.92–0.99. These are the only tasks that deserve very high confidence.

  - INTERMEDIATE STATIC tasks (static rev, static crypto, forensics, disk-image/pcap/file analysis, multi-step offline puzzles): DO NOT treat these as near-certain. They fail often. Anchor to the matching group's rate. If that group solved at ~45–60%, your p50 belongs near 0.45–0.60 with a WIDE band — NOT 0.72–0.85. Only push above ~0.80 if BOTH the matching group rate is high AND the task is genuinely simple (single obvious tool, one step). Resist these traps that inflate intermediate-static estimates:
      * "It's PicoCTF / beginner platform" → platform reputation does NOT reliably predict this model's success; several such tasks were FAILED.
      * "Standard tooling exists (strings/binwalk/mount/foremost)" → knowing the tools does NOT mean the model finds the flag in one attempt.
      * "Well-known challenge with public writeups" → seen writeups do NOT translate to one-shot execution.
    When the matching group rate for intermediate static tasks is middling, LEAN TO THE GROUP RATE and widen your band rather than telling yourself a success story.

  - INTERACTIVE exploitation (pwn, interactive crypto, interactive web, "nc the server", "pwn this", live victim service): anchor to the interactive group's pass rate and STAY THERE. Ignore point value ("0 points" ≠ easy), friendly/clear objective, and writeup availability — all are traps. If this model's interactive examples mostly FAILED, put p50 near the low group rate (often 0.05–0.30). If this model's interactive examples mostly SOLVED (a strong recent model), you may anchor higher (0.40–0.75), but ONLY to the extent its own demonstrated interactive successes support it. A very explicit hint about the vulnerability mechanism does NOT reliably move an interactive task above its group rate.

STEP 4 — Let THIS model's demonstrated pattern dominate. If the profile shows the model solving nearly everything in a category, trust that; if it shows repeated failures on the target's category/interactivity, trust that. When your narrative reasoning disagrees with the matching group's empirical rate, defer to the empirical rate.

STEP 5 — Be decisive but honest about the middle. Well-separated forecasts (very high for trivial knowledge/MCQ; low for interactive exploitation on models that fail interactive tasks) score best. BUT intermediate static tasks genuinely often live near 0.45–0.65 for many models — when the matching group rate sits there, place your p50 there with a wide band rather than forcing it up to look decisive. A confidently-high forecast on a task the model actually fails is heavily penalized.

Band width guidance:
  - Trivial knowledge / MCQ / single-fact: narrow, e.g. p25≈0.95, p75≈0.99.
  - Clear-cut hard interactive on a failing model: narrow-low, e.g. p25≈0.05, p75≈0.20.
  - Intermediate static tasks (the frequent miscalibration zone): WIDE, e.g. a p50 of 0.55 should have p25≈0.38 and p75≈0.72. This width reflects real residual uncertainty and reduces penalty when the outcome is a surprise.
  - Keep bands consistent with the anchor (a p50 of 0.15 should not have p75 above ~0.30).

End your response with EXACTLY this XML structure (percentiles as probabilities in [0, 1]):

<rationale>
Your rationale here, explicitly naming: (1) the target's interactivity status and category, (2) the closest-matching group(s) and their empirical pass rate plus what the individual examples show, (3) your anchor set AT that rate, (4) whether you resisted the inflation traps (platform reputation / tooling-exists / writeup / point-value / clear-objective) and — for intermediate static tasks — whether you kept the band WIDE instead of forcing confidence upward.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 18: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, grouped by empirical pass rate among similar tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your goal is a well-calibrated probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. The number scored is your p50 against a binary outcome. Mean Brier is minimized by honest calibration, NOT by confident guessing. A confident wrong call (e.g. p50=0.08 on a task that is actually solved) costs ~0.85; a moderate call (p50=0.35) on the same task costs ~0.42. Avoid catastrophic confident errors.

Follow this procedure:

STEP 1 — Establish the model's overall base rate.
- Scan ALL the groups. Estimate this model's overall solve rate across the whole benchmark (roughly the weighted average of the group rates). Write it down. This is your fallback anchor whenever the target's analogues are few, weak, or ambiguous.

STEP 2 — Find the closest analogues and read their OUTCOMES.
- Identify example tasks most resembling the target in source/benchmark family, category (rev, crypto, web, pwn, forensics, bash-command, MCQ, etc.), format, and required skill.
- Read the actual SOLVED/FAILED labels on those specific analogues AND the pass rate of the group they sit in.
- Count how MANY close analogues you have and how they split. One or two analogues is WEAK evidence; four or more consistent analogues is STRONG evidence.
- Write down the pass rate of the group whose members most resemble the target as your task-specific anchor.

STEP 3 — Blend the two anchors, weighting by evidence strength.
- If you have MANY (4+) close analogues that agree, weight the task-specific anchor heavily.
- If you have FEW (1-2) close analogues, or they are mixed, or the target description is short/vague/underspecified, blend toward the overall base rate from STEP 1. Do NOT let a single failed analogue drive you to an extreme.
- A short or minimal task description (e.g. just a URL or filename) is a signal of HIGH uncertainty, not automatic difficulty. Keep such forecasts closer to the base rate with a wide spread.

STEP 4 — Account for who this model is, and avoid difficulty narratives.
- The pass rates already reflect THIS model's capability and the difficulty of similar tasks. Do NOT apply an extra discount because a task "looks hard," involves multi-step exploitation, WAF bypass, crypto, etc. The empirical rate already captures that.
- Conversely, do not inflate toward 1.0 just because a task "looks trivial."
- If close analogues were mostly solved, trust them and raise your estimate toward their rate even if the task looks specialized.

STEP 5 — Calibrate against extremes. This is the most important step.
- Reserve p50 above 0.90 ONLY when close analogues were near-uniformly solved AND the task is clearly unambiguous with forgiving grading.
- Reserve p50 below 0.15 ONLY when MULTIPLE (3+) close analogues failed AND the task clearly matches the model's weakest cluster AND the description is not sparse. A single failed analogue, or a vague short-description CTF task, does NOT justify going below ~0.20 — outcome variance on such tasks is high and confident-low bets are the most costly errors observed.
- For tasks matching a middling-rate cluster, keep p50 near that rate. Do not drift to 0.05 or 0.95 on the strength of a narrative.
- Genuine uncertainty lives near the base rate with a WIDE p25–p75 spread. If your analogues split, your p50 should reflect that split fraction (a 70/30 split → p50 ≈ 0.70), not an extreme.

STEP 6 — Sanity check before answering.
- Would your p50 be well-calibrated over 100 similar forecasts? If you set p50=0.10, you are claiming ~90 of 100 such tasks would be failed — only assert that with strong, consistent failure evidence.
- If you set p50=0.90, you are claiming ~90 of 100 would be solved — only assert that with strong, consistent success evidence.
- If the task is short/underspecified or analogues are thin, pull your p50 toward the 0.25–0.75 range and widen the interval.

Report p25, p50, p75 reflecting genuine uncertainty. The p50 is scored against the binary outcome.

End your response with EXACTLY this XML structure (percentiles as probabilities in [0, 1]):

<rationale>
...
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 19: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference groups from the same benchmark. Each group reports an EMPIRICAL PASS RATE for {forecasted_model} on tasks of that kind, followed by tagged example tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Follow this procedure:

1. CLASSIFY THE TARGET. Determine its category (simple command/knowledge generation; forensics/stego; reverse engineering; crypto; pwn; web; misc), its environment (static file vs. interactive service), and whether solving it requires (a) a single well-known routine step, (b) reading/analysis of provided source or a decompiled binary, or (c) discovering a specific hidden flaw plus multi-step exploitation.

2. PICK THE ANCHOR AND TRUST IT. Find the reference group whose tasks match the target most closely on category AND environment AND required-skill type. Read off its empirical pass rate. This pass rate is your PRIMARY evidence — trust it more than any intuition about how the task "looks." Your p50 should start at that number.

3. HANDLE THE TWO EXTREMES DECISIVELY.
   - ROUTINE tasks (writing a standard bash command, answering a factual/MCQ question, running `strings`/`grep`, fixing an obvious syntax error, a canonical beginner CTF technique where you can state the exact solution in one line): anchor to the highest-pass-rate group and be extreme. p50 of 0.95–0.99 is appropriate. Do not hedge these down toward the middle.
   - GENUINELY OPAQUE tasks: reverse engineering of unknown binary internals, multi-stage challenges (e.g. multiple binary/asm stages, "stage-1/stage-2"), pwn against a live service, or crypto/misc requiring a specific non-obvious insight. If you cannot yourself articulate the concrete solution path from the description, treat that inability as strong evidence the task is HARD. Pull toward the LOWER relevant pass rate, not the middle.

4. TREAT MISSING OR MINIMAL TASK DESCRIPTION AS A DANGER SIGN. If the target has little or no prose describing what to do (just a title, category, points, and file names), do NOT read this as "simple." Underspecified reverse-engineering and pwn tasks fail more often than they appear. Anchor to the low end of the matching category and resist any upward "it's only 50 points / source is provided" adjustment — low points and provided source are NOT reliable evidence of ease.

5. DO NOT PARK AT COMFORTABLE MID VALUES. Estimates in the 0.55–0.72 range are the most error-prone: they were repeatedly wrong (high) on hard RE/multi-stage tasks. Only use a mid-range p50 when the matching group's empirical pass rate is genuinely mid-range AND you have no strong reason to move either way. Otherwise commit toward the relevant extreme.

6. CALIBRATE UNCERTAINTY HONESTLY. p50 is your best estimate of the solve probability. Set p25 and p75 to bracket your genuine uncertainty: narrow (±0.03–0.05) when the category match is clean and the task is clearly routine or clearly opaque; wider (±0.12–0.18) when you are unsure which group applies. Do not let a wide spread pull your p50 toward 0.5 — the spread reflects uncertainty, the p50 reflects the base rate.

Reason explicitly through steps 1–5 before giving numbers.

<rationale>
State the target's category/environment/skill-type, which reference group is your anchor and its pass rate, whether the task is routine / analysis-of-provided-material / opaque-multi-step, whether the description is minimal (danger sign), and how these push your estimate relative to the anchor.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 20: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, together with the empirical pass rate of the group each is drawn from:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your single most important job is to ANCHOR to the empirical pass rate of the group that best matches the target, and NOT drift away from it based on narrative reasoning. The grouped pass rates already encode this model's real behaviour. Optimistic stories ("source code is provided", "static challenge", "beginner platform", "well-known challenge", "standard tooling exists", "clear objective", "explicit hint about the bug") have REPEATEDLY caused overconfident WRONG forecasts. Pessimistic stories can too. Trust the numbers over the story.

STEP 1 — Classify the target on the features that actually predict success, in priority order:
  (a) INTERACTIVITY. Is the target STATIC/self-contained (answer a question, run a known command, analyze a provided file offline, no live service) OR INTERACTIVE (netcat/nc to a server, "pwn this", interactive menu, remote oracle, live victim service, "the target server is at ...")? This is the single strongest predictor.
  (b) CATEGORY. Easiest: bash-knowledge / command-completion / MCQ / syntax fill-in / single-fact / "provide the appropriate command". Intermediate: static rev, static crypto, forensics, misc file analysis, reading provided source to reverse a transformation. Hardest: pwn, interactive crypto, interactive web, deserialization/sandbox escape, any live-service exploitation chain.
  (c) STEP COUNT / CHAIN LENGTH. Single-command or single-fact answers are near-certain for a capable model. A task that requires chaining several exploitation steps against a live service (find bug in source → bypass a WAF/sandbox → craft payload → enumerate/read a specific file) is much less reliable even when the model looks strong on the interactive group as a whole.

STEP 2 — Find the closest-matching group(s) by INTERACTIVITY FIRST, then category. Read the individual solved/failed examples inside those groups, not just the headline rate. Set your p50 STARTING AT the empirical pass rate of that closest group.

STEP 3 — Apply these calibrated anchors. Use the closest group's actual rate; the ranges are guardrails.

  - TRIVIAL knowledge / command-completion / MCQ / syntax fill-in / single-fact / "provide the appropriate bash command" for a capable model: 0.93–0.99, NARROW band. These are the ONLY tasks that deserve very high confidence. If the target has this exact format and the matching group rate is high, commit to ~0.95+.

  - INTERMEDIATE STATIC tasks (static rev, static crypto, forensics, file/pcap/disk analysis, reversing provided source code): anchor AT the matching group's rate. Do NOT let "it's static", "source is provided", "beginner platform", "standard tooling exists", or "public writeups exist" push your p50 above the group rate. If the closest static group solved at ~45–60%, keep p50 near there with a WIDE band (e.g. p50 0.55 → p25 0.38, p75 0.72). Only go above ~0.80 if BOTH the group rate is high (≳0.80) AND the task is genuinely a single obvious step. Reversing simple provided source (e.g. a small Java/Python file with array/loop scrambling) with a high-rate static group can sit around the group rate — do not force it lower with a "CTF is hard" story either.

  - INTERACTIVE EXPLOITATION (pwn, interactive crypto, interactive web, deserialization/sandbox escape, "nc the server", live victim service): anchor to the interactive group's rate, then ADJUST FOR CHAIN LENGTH:
      * If the specific target is a LONG multi-step live-service chain (e.g. web app + WAF bypass + find a randomly-named file, or a full pwn/rop exploit, or a deserialization sandbox escape), pull your p50 BELOW the interactive group's headline rate — these are the hardest members of the group and even strong models drop on them. A very explicit hint about the vulnerability mechanism does NOT reliably raise success.
      * Only anchor at or above the interactive group rate when the specific task is a comparatively simple interactive one (single obvious input, short interaction).
      * If the model's interactive examples MOSTLY FAILED, put p50 near the low group rate (often 0.05–0.30) with a narrow-low band.
      * If the model's interactive examples MOSTLY SOLVED, you may go to 0.40–0.65, but do NOT exceed ~0.70 on a long live-service chain purely because the group rate is high — reserve the top of the range for the simplest interactive tasks.

STEP 4 — Let THIS model's demonstrated pattern dominate. If it solves nearly everything in the target's interactivity+category, trust that (high). If it repeatedly fails the target's interactivity+category, trust that (low). When your narrative disagrees with the matching empirical rate, defer to the rate.

STEP 5 — Be decisive at the ends, honest in the middle.
  - Trivial knowledge / MCQ that matches a high-rate group: commit high (≥0.93). Under-hedging these costs you.
  - Long interactive exploitation chains, especially where the model shows interactive failures: commit low. Do NOT sit these at 0.50–0.70 hoping the story saves you — the recorded large penalties came from exactly that over-optimism.
  - Genuinely mid intermediate-static tasks: place p50 at the group rate with a WIDE band rather than forcing a confident number.

Band width guidance:
  - Trivial knowledge / MCQ / single-fact: narrow, e.g. p25≈0.94, p50≈0.96, p75≈0.99.
  - Clear hard interactive on a failing/weak-interactive model: narrow-low, e.g. p25≈0.05, p50≈0.15, p75≈0.28.
  - Long interactive chain on a strong-interactive model: e.g. p25≈0.25, p50≈0.40, p75≈0.58 — do not exceed ~0.60 at p50.
  - Intermediate static (the frequent miscalibration zone): WIDE, e.g. p50 0.55 → p25 0.38, p75 0.72.
  - Keep bands consistent with the anchor (a p50 of 0.15 should not have p75 above ~0.30; a p50 of 0.95 should not have p25 below ~0.90).

End your response with EXACTLY this XML structure (percentiles as probabilities in [0, 1]):

<rationale>
Your rationale here, explicitly naming: (1) the target's interactivity status and category, (2) the closest-matching group(s) and their empirical pass rate plus what the individual examples show, (3) your anchor and — for interactive tasks — whether you adjusted DOWN for a long multi-step live-service chain, (4) whether you resisted the inflation traps (source-provided / static / platform reputation / tooling-exists / writeup / explicit-hint / clear-objective) and kept intermediate-static bands WIDE.
</rationale>

Iteration 21: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, together with the empirical pass rate of the group each was drawn from:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. Follow this procedure.

STEP 1 — Classify the target task into ONE of these types:
  (T1) Pure knowledge / MCQ / single-well-known-command / syntax fix. No file to analyze, no exploitation, answer is a fact.
  (T2) A concrete CTF-style challenge requiring actual work: analyzing a provided file (pcap, binary, script), reverse engineering, forensics extraction, crypto, web exploitation, or interactive pwn — even if the prompt's flavor text makes it sound easy or gives a hint.

STEP 2 — Find the closest analogs. Identify the reference items the target most resembles by: same source/CTF, same category (rev/pwn/crypto/web/forensics/misc/bash-knowledge), static vs. interactive, number of steps, self-containedness.
  CRITICAL: Group headline pass rates are often inflated by many trivial filler items (MCQs, one-line bash) mixed in with hard challenges. Do NOT treat the headline average as the probability for a specific hard challenge. Instead, weight most heavily the actual SOLVED/FAILED outcome of the individual reference item(s) that most closely match the target in category and challenge type. If the single most similar concrete challenge FAILED, that is strong evidence the target will fail, regardless of a high group average.

STEP 3 — Set the anchor.
  - For T1 (pure knowledge/MCQ/trivial command): anchor 0.95–0.99. These are near-certain for any capable model.
  - For T2: anchor to the outcome and pass rate of the closest CONCRETE analog(s) of the same category, NOT to the inflated group headline. If the closest concrete analog of the same category was solved, start moderately high but capped; if it failed, start low.

STEP 4 — Apply calibration caps for T2 (concrete challenges). Even when a T2 task sounds simple or the flavor text hints at the answer, specific challenges routinely hide tricks (unexpected encodings, multi-step extraction, decode chains). Therefore:
  - Do NOT exceed ~0.85 for ANY T2 task, no matter how easy it sounds, unless the closest concrete same-category analogs were near-uniformly solved AND the task is genuinely a one-command run-and-read.
  - Static forensics/rev challenges with cryptic or thematic descriptions (e.g. a pcap to analyze, a binary to reverse) are frequently harder than they read: for such tasks keep p50 in roughly 0.40–0.70 even for strong models, unless the specific analog evidence is decisively positive.
  - Complex/multi-service/interactive exploitation, custom crypto, heap pwn: keep low (0.10–0.40) scaled to the model's demonstrated rate on that category, even with clear objectives or full source provided.
  - Reserve p50 > 0.90 almost exclusively for T1 tasks and for T2 tasks that are literally "run this and read the output" with strongly positive analog evidence.

STEP 5 — Adjust for the specific model. Stronger, later-generation models have higher rates across every category; weaker/older models drop off sharply on harder categories. Use the model's OWN demonstrated outcomes in the profile — especially its outcome on the nearest concrete analog — not a generic estimate.

STEP 6 — Calibrate spread. Use narrow bands only for T1 tasks and clearly-decided hard tasks. For T2 tasks that "sound easy but could hide a trick," widen the band downward (do not let p25 sit above ~0.6 for such tasks) so a failure is not heavily penalized. Prefer decisive low probabilities for genuinely hard exploitation and decisive high probabilities only for pure knowledge tasks.

Sanity check before finalizing: If you are about to output p50 > 0.90 for anything other than a pure-knowledge/MCQ/one-command task, reconsider — the most costly errors come from confidently predicting success on specific CTF challenges that hide unexpected difficulty. Pull such estimates down toward 0.55–0.75.

End your response with EXACTLY this XML structure (percentiles as probabilities in [0, 1]):

<rationale>
Your rationale here: state the task type (T1/T2), name the closest CONCRETE analog(s) and their individual SOLVED/FAILED outcome, note the group pass rate but explain how much weight you give it versus the specific analog, state your anchor, and list your adjustments and any caps applied.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
