Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, grouped by empirical pass rate among similar tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your goal is a well-calibrated probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. The number that is scored is your p50 against a binary outcome, so calibration beats confidence. The empirical pass rates shown ALREADY reflect this model's true capability and the true difficulty of similar tasks — they are your primary evidence. Your job is mainly to locate the target among the shown groups, not to re-derive difficulty from scratch. Follow this procedure.

STEP 1 — Identify the closest analogues and their outcomes.
- Find the example tasks most similar in SOURCE (CTF/benchmark family), CATEGORY (rev, crypto, web, pwn, forensics, misc, bash-command, MCQ, etc.), FORMAT, and required skill.
- Note their actual SOLVED/FAILED labels AND the empirical pass rate of the group they belong to. Write down that rate explicitly as your ANCHOR.
- If several close analogues were SOLVED, that is strong positive evidence even if the task looks specialized or multi-step. If several were FAILED, that is strong negative evidence.

STEP 2 — Adjust the anchor for TRACTABILITY relative to the analogues, in BOTH directions.
- Compare the target's format to the closest analogues. Some formats are systematically EASIER than others: static/offline challenges (no live service) are easier than interactive ones; tasks that hand you the algorithm/source/hint explicitly are easier than tasks where you must discover it; short factual/command/MCQ completions are easy.
- If your closest analogues are HARDER in format than the target (e.g. the analogue was an interactive oracle challenge but the target is a static source-provided reverse task), the anchor UNDERSTATES the target — raise your estimate ABOVE the anchor. Do NOT anchor on a harder-format neighbor and then sit at its rate.
- If your closest analogues are EASIER in format than the target, lower your estimate below the anchor.
- If format is comparable, stay at the anchor.

STEP 3 — Do NOT apply a generic difficulty discount.
- A narrative like "this involves pickle escapes / lattice crypto / multi-step exploitation, so it must be hard" is NOT a reason to go below the anchor when the closest analogues of that type were solved. The empirical rate already prices in that difficulty.
- If you catch yourself writing "this looks really hard for a model" while the matching analogues were mostly solved, delete that reasoning and move your estimate TOWARD the analogue rate.
- Conversely, do not push a genuinely hard task up on optimism if its closest analogues actually failed.

STEP 4 — Short-answer / exact-match caution.
- For short knowledge/command/completion/MCQ tasks with strict grading, only exceed 0.90 when the closest analogues were near-uniformly solved AND the answer is unambiguous. Reserve a little probability for format/exact-match slips.
- If a directly analogous short item FAILED, pull toward the group rate, not toward 1.0.

STEP 5 — Calibrate to the empirical evidence, honestly.
- Sanity check: if you made 100 forecasts like this, would p50 match the observed solve fraction of the closest analogues (after the tractability adjustment)? If analogues split ~70/30 solved, p50 belongs near 0.70, not 0.40 or 0.95.
- Use these ranges as discipline:
  * Closest analogues nearly all solved AND task clearly tractable → 0.85–0.95.
  * Closest analogues mostly solved, comparable format → near the anchor (often 0.6–0.85).
  * Closest analogues mixed → middling p50 near the anchor with a WIDE p25–p75.
  * Closest analogues mostly failed → 0.15–0.40 depending on tractability.
  * Closest analogues near-uniformly failed → 0.05–0.15.
- Avoid reflexive extreme values. Genuine uncertainty lives near the base rate, not at 0.05 or 0.95.

STEP 6 — Report p25, p50, p75 reflecting genuine uncertainty. Make p50 match the empirical analogue evidence (adjusted for tractability), not your gut feeling about how hard the task feels. Widen the p25–p75 band when analogues are mixed or sparse.

Iteration 39: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference groups from the same benchmark. Each group reports an EMPIRICAL PASS RATE for {forecasted_model} on tasks of that kind, followed by tagged example tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Your goal is a well-calibrated probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. The single most important fact is this: the empirical pass rate of the best-matching reference group is a MUCH better predictor than your intuition about how the task "looks." Forecasts that trust the matching pass rate consistently beat forecasts that adjust it based on task appearance. Adjustments away from the matching pass rate — in EITHER direction — are the main source of error. Keep them small and only when clearly justified.

Follow this procedure.

1. CLASSIFY THE TARGET on three axes:
   - Category: simple command/knowledge generation; forensics/stego; reverse engineering; crypto; pwn; web; misc.
   - Environment: static file(s) vs. interactive/live service.
   - Required skill: (a) a single well-known routine step statable in one line; (b) reading/analysis of provided source or a decompiled binary; (c) discovering a specific hidden flaw plus multi-step exploitation.

2. FIND THE MATCHING GROUP AND READ OFF ITS RATE. Pick the reference group whose example tasks are closest to the target on category, environment, AND required-skill type. Read its empirical pass rate. This rate IS your starting p50. Write it down explicitly. If two groups plausibly match, take a weighted blend of their rates (weighted toward the closer match) rather than defaulting to the midpoint between them.

3. ADJUST ONLY A LITTLE, AND ONLY WITH A CONCRETE REASON. Your final p50 should normally stay within about ±0.08 of the matching group's rate. Move further ONLY if the target is unambiguously different in kind from the group's examples. Specifically:
   - Do NOT push a moderate matching rate (say 0.4–0.8) up toward 0.9+ just because the task "sounds routine." Many plausibly-routine tasks still fail. If the matching group's rate is moderate, your p50 stays moderate.
   - Do NOT push a high matching rate down toward the middle just because you can name an extra complication (multi-part, custom implementation, "needs insight"). If the matching group already contains comparably complex tasks, its rate already reflects that complexity.
   - Only anchor to a very high value (0.93–0.99) when BOTH the matching group's rate is itself very high AND the task is genuinely a one-line, canonical, unambiguous action (a standard command, an MCQ/factual answer, `strings`/`grep`, a trivial syntax fix). If the matching group's rate is not itself very high, do not go to 0.93+ no matter how easy the task looks.
   - Only anchor to a very low value (0.05–0.20) when BOTH the matching group's rate is itself low AND the task is genuinely opaque: live-service pwn, multi-stage RE, or crypto/misc needing a specific non-obvious insight you cannot articulate.

4. MINIMAL DESCRIPTIONS ARE NOT EVIDENCE OF EASE. A bare title plus file names for an RE/pwn/crypto task is not "simple." Do not adjust upward for low points, provided source, or terse prompts — these are not reliable signs of ease. When the description is minimal for a hard category, stay at or slightly below the matching group's rate.

5. AVOID BOTH EXTREMES OF ERROR. The most damaging mistakes seen historically were confident forecasts (0.85+) on tasks that failed, and confident-low forecasts (below 0.2) on tasks that succeeded. Before committing, sanity-check: "Is my p50 far from the matching group's rate? If so, do I have a concrete, specific reason, or am I just reacting to how the task looks?" If the latter, move back toward the group's rate.

6. CALIBRATE THE SPREAD. p50 is your best estimate. Set p25 and p75 to bracket genuine uncertainty: narrow (±0.03–0.06) when the category/environment/skill match is clean; wider (±0.10–0.18) when you are unsure which group applies or the task straddles groups. The spread reflects uncertainty; it must not drag p50 toward 0.5 — keep p50 at the matching rate (lightly adjusted).

In your rationale, explicitly state: the target's category/environment/skill-type; which reference group matches and its exact empirical pass rate; whether the task is routine / analysis-of-provided-material / opaque-multi-step; whether the description is minimal for a hard category; the size and direction of any adjustment you make and the concrete reason for it; and confirm your final p50 is close to the matching group's rate unless a specific reason justifies otherwise.

Iteration 40: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference groups from the same benchmark. Each group reports an EMPIRICAL PASS RATE for {forecasted_model} on tasks of that kind, followed by tagged example tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt. The outcome is BINARY (solved / not solved) on ONE attempt, so even "obvious" tasks fail sometimes and even "hard" tasks succeed sometimes. Your goal is a well-calibrated probability, not a confident guess. Because the score penalises confident mistakes heavily, reserve extreme probabilities for cases where you are genuinely almost certain.

Follow this procedure:

1. CLASSIFY THE TARGET. Note its category (simple command / knowledge / MCQ; forensics/stego; reverse engineering; crypto; pwn; web; misc) and environment (static file vs. interactive service).

2. PICK THE ANCHOR AND TRUST IT. Find the reference group whose tasks most closely match the target on category AND environment, for the model whose behaviour you are forecasting. Read off its empirical pass rate. This is your p50 starting point and, in most cases, close to your final p50. If several groups plausibly apply, interpolate between their pass rates using the group with the closest example tasks. The empirical pass rate already reflects difficulty, cryptic titles, minimal descriptions, high point values, live services, and opaque exploitation — do NOT discount again for these. Double-counting them is the single most common calibration error.

3. MINIMAL OR CRYPTIC DESCRIPTIONS ARE NOT INFORMATIVE ON THEIR OWN. Do not read "No description provided," flavor text, or a terse one-liner as evidence of difficulty OR of ease. Rely on the category+environment pass rate.

4. HANDLE THE GENUINELY UNAMBIGUOUS ROUTINE CASE — CAREFULLY.
   A task qualifies as "unambiguously routine" ONLY if BOTH hold:
     (a) it is a standard bash command, a canonical one-line technique, an obvious syntax fix, running strings/grep, OR a factual/MCQ question with a SINGLE universally-agreed correct answer (e.g. "standard SSH port = 22"); AND
     (b) the matching group's pass rate is already high.
   For such tasks set p50 in the 0.92–0.97 range (not higher — single-attempt binary outcomes still fail occasionally).
   CRITICAL EXCEPTION — AMBIGUOUS OR DEBATABLE MCQs: if a multiple-choice or short-answer question has MORE THAN ONE defensible answer, depends on unstated context, tests an arbitrary convention, or hinges on what the author "intended," it is NOT unambiguously routine even though it "looks" trivial. These are effectively coin-flips-plus that different capable models get right or wrong inconsistently. Cap p50 for such questions at roughly 0.55–0.70 and widen the bands. Do NOT push them to 0.95. (A question like "What is THE config file extension?" with several plausible options — .conf, .cfg, .cnf, .config — is exactly this ambiguous case.)

5. STAY AT THE ANCHOR FOR EVERYTHING ELSE. For any task that is not unambiguously routine, set p50 to the matching group's empirical pass rate. Mid-range values (0.30–0.70) are CORRECT when the evidence is mid-range: do not flee toward 0 or 1, and do not flee toward 0.5 either. Do not invent an upward or downward adjustment because the task "looks" hard or easy.

6. RESPECT SOFT BOUNDS TO CONTROL TAIL PENALTIES.
   - Do not push p50 above ~0.93 unless the task is unambiguously routine (per step 4) AND the matching group's pass rate is near 1.0.
   - Do not push p50 below ~0.12 unless the matching group's pass rate is genuinely near zero AND the task is clearly in a hardest-style category. Keep a floor reflecting that capable models occasionally solve even hard tasks.
   - When the matching group's pass rate is high but not near-perfect (say 0.80–0.90) and the task is NOT unambiguously routine, keep p50 at the anchor rather than rounding up toward 0.95 — a single failed high-confidence forecast is very costly.

7. CALIBRATE UNCERTAINTY.
   - p25 and p75 bracket genuine uncertainty; they must not drag p50 away from the empirical anchor.
   - Use a NARROW spread (±0.03–0.06) only when the category+environment match is clean AND the task is either unambiguously routine or the anchor is very extreme (near 0 or near 1).
   - Use a WIDER spread (±0.10–0.15) when two groups plausibly apply, the match is loose, the anchor is mid-range (0.3–0.7), or the task is an ambiguous MCQ. Single-attempt binary outcomes with a mid-range anchor carry real variance — do not under-state it.

Reason explicitly through steps 1–7. State the target's category/environment, the matching group and its pass rate, whether the task is unambiguously routine / an ambiguous-looking-easy MCQ / neither, and confirm you did not double-count difficulty or over-confidently extreme-ify.

<rationale>
State the target's category and environment, which reference group is your anchor and its empirical pass rate, whether the task is unambiguously routine, an ambiguous MCQ that only looks easy, or neither, and how you arrived at a p50 that stays close to the empirical anchor while avoiding unwarranted extreme confidence.
</rationale>
